Matching logic - a new axiomatization
Laurenţiu Leuştean, Dafina Trufaş, [2mm] Institute for Logic, Data Science, Bucharest

Abstract




Chapter 1
Introduction

Matching logic was introduced by Grigore Roşu
[
15
,
14
]
. Different versions
of matching logic were recently studied (see, for example,
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
3
]
).

In Chapter
2
we propose a new proof system for
appML
, first-order matching logic with application.
This proof system, denoted
\mathcal{G}^{c}
, is obtained by adapting to matching logic Gödel’s proof sytem for
first-order intuitionistic logic (see
[
7
,
18
]
). We prove different useful theorems and derived rules,
important properties of the proof system. For different proofs in the propositional
fragment of
\mathcal{G}^{c}
, Kleene’s textbook
[
9
]
was a source of inspiration.

Finally, we prove that
\mathcal{G}^{c}
is equivalent with the proof system
\mathcal{H}
for appML, obtained
from the proof system defined in
[
1
]
by considering only the axioms and deduction rules from the categories
FOL reasoning and Frame reasoning.

In Chapter
3
we give a new, simpler proof system
\mathcal{T}_{def}
for first-order matching
logic with application and definedness (
appML_{def}
​). The proof system
\mathcal{T}_{def}
is inspired by
Tarski’s
[
17
,
16
]
axiomatization for first order-logic with equality (simplified by Kalish and
Montague
[
8
,
13
]
), that does not involve the notions of a free variable and free substitution.
We prove different useful theorems and derived rules for the propositional and first-order connectives,
as well as for definedness, equality, totality, membership and application (contexts). Some of the results
are obtained by adapting to our setting proofs from
[
12
, Chapter 10]
. The propositional axioms
and deduction rules of

\mathcal{T}_{def}
coincide with the ones of the proof system
\mathcal{G}^{c}
, hence we can use in this setting
propositional theorems and derived deduction rules from Chapter
2
.

We point out the following important results:

(i)

replacement theorems (Subsection
3.1.6
);

(ii)

substitution of equal variables (Subsection
3.2.1
);

(iii)

the bounded substitution theorem (Subsection
3.2.2
);

(iv)

universal and existential specification (Subsection
3.2.3
);

(v)

substitution of equal patterns (Subsection
3.2.7
);

(vi)

the deduction theorem (Subsection
3.2.8
);

(vii)

the propagation and framing rules from the proof system for applicative matching logic used in

[
4
,
3
,
2
]
(Subsection
3.2.9
);

(viii)

theorems and rules for definedness, totality, equality (Subsections
3.1.3
,
3.2.5
)
and membership (Subsection
3.2.10
).

Theorem
3.3.1
, the main result of Section
3.3
, shows that
\mathcal{G}^{c}

is weaker than
\mathcal{T}_{def}
. As
\mathcal{G}^{c}
is equivalent with
\mathcal{H}
, we get that
\mathcal{H}
is
also weaker than
\mathcal{T}_{def}
. By using notions and results from
[
11
]
, we prove in Section
3.4
a soundness
theorem for
\mathcal{T}_{def}
.

Throughout these notes, we use the terminology for abstract matching logic from
[
10
]
. Thus, we refer
to
[
10
]
for definitions and properties of free and bound variables,
free substitution
Subf
and bounded substitution
Subb
, variable free for patterns,
congruences, contexts, proof systems, as well as for all the undefined notions from these notes.

Chapter 2
Applicative matching logic with application
appML

The logic
appML
is defined as follows:

(i)

\mathcal{L}_{appML}
:

\mathcal{P}_{C}=\{\bot\}
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\{\neg\}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\{\to,\wedge,\vee,\cdot\}
,

\mathcal{Q}=\{\forall,\exists\}
,
Equal={\overline{\mathcal{Q}}}=SVar=\emptyset
.

(ii)

Its proof system,
\mathcal{G}^{c}
, is given in Appendix
A
.

We write simply
\vdash
instead of
\vdash_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}
.

We shall use the infix notation for patterns. Thus, we write
\varphi\to\psi
instead of
\to\varphi\psi
,

\varphi\wedge\psi
instead of
\wedge\varphi\psi
,
\varphi\vee\psi
instead of
\vee\varphi\psi
,

{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}
instead of
\cdot\varphi\psi
.
We also use parentheses when it is necessary to make clear the
structure of a pattern. For example, we write
(\varphi\to\psi)\to\chi
.

We introduce the derived connective
\leftrightarrow
by the following abbreviation

\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi:=(\varphi\to\psi)\wedge(\psi\to\varphi).

To reduce the use of parentheses, we assume that

(i)

quantifiers
\exists
,
\forall
, application,
\neg
have higher precedence
than
\rightarrow
,
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\leftrightarrow
;

(ii)

\wedge,\vee
have higher precedence than
\rightarrow
,
\leftrightarrow
.

2.1
Theorems and derived rules in
\mathcal{G}^{c}

In the following,
\Gamma
is an arbitrary set of patterns and
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\chi
,
\varphi^{\prime}
,
\psi^{\prime}
are arbitrary patterns.

2.1.1
Propositional connectives

Lemma 2.1.1
.

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\psi\to\varphi\vee\psi,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\wedge\psi\to\psi,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\to(\psi\to\varphi),

(2.3)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\to\varphi,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\to(\psi\to\varphi\wedge\psi),

(2.5)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,(\varphi\to\psi)\wedge\varphi\to\psi,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\wedge(\varphi\to\psi)\to\psi,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\to((\varphi\to\psi)\to\psi),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,(\varphi\wedge\psi)\wedge\chi\to\varphi,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\wedge(\psi\wedge\chi)\to\psi,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,(\varphi\wedge\psi)\wedge\chi\to\psi,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\varphi\wedge(\psi\wedge\chi)\to\chi,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\psi\to\varphi\vee(\psi\vee\chi),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\chi\to\varphi\vee(\psi\vee\chi),

(2.14)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\varphi\to(\varphi\vee\psi)\vee\chi,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\psi\to(\varphi\vee\psi)\vee\chi.

(2.16)

Proof.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\to\varphi,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi

iff

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\wedge\psi,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\vee\chi\to\psi\vee\chi,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\vee\psi\to\psi\text{~and~}\Gamma\vdash\psi\vee\varphi\to\psi,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\wedge\chi\to\psi\text{~and~}\Gamma\vdash\chi\wedge\varphi\to\psi,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\chi

iff

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\wedge\chi,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\chi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\to\chi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\vee\psi\to\chi,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\chi\to\varphi)\to(\chi\to\psi),

(2.24)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\wedge\chi\to\psi\wedge\gamma,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\vee\chi\to\psi\vee\gamma,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\wedge\psi\to\chi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\wedge\varphi\to\chi,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\wedge\varphi\to\chi,

(2.28)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\to(\varphi\to\chi),

(2.29)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\wedge\chi\to\psi\wedge\chi

(2.30)

\displaystyle\text{~and~}\Gamma\vdash\chi\wedge\varphi\to\chi\wedge\psi,

(2.31)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\psi\to\chi)\to(\varphi\to\chi),

(2.32)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\varphi\wedge\psi\text{~and~}\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\wedge\varphi,

(2.33)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\chi,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\gamma).

(2.35)

Proof.

Lemma 2.1.5
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\varphi\to\psi)\to(\varphi\to\chi)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\gamma),

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

Lemma 2.1.11
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\varphi,

(2.83)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\chi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\chi,

(2.84)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\vee\psi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\vee\psi^{\prime},

(2.85)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\wedge\psi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\wedge\psi^{\prime},

(2.86)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime},

(2.87)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\text{~and~}\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\varphi^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime})

(2.88)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi,

(2.89)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi^{\prime},

(2.90)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\prime},\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}.

(2.91)

(Assumption)

2.1.2
First-order connectives

Lemma 2.1.19
.

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\forall x\varphi\to\varphi,

(2.125)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\varphi\to\exists x\varphi,

(2.126)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\forall x\varphi\to\exists x\varphi.

(2.127)

Proof.

(
2.125
): Apply
(
\forall
-Quantifier)
with
y:=x
, as
x
is free
for
x
in
\varphi
and
Subf^{x}_{x}{\varphi}=\varphi
.

(
2.126
): Apply
(
\exists
-Quantifier)
with
y:=x
,
x
is free
for
x
in
\varphi
and

.

(

):

(2)

(
2.126
)

(3)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

∎

Lemma 2.1.24
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be element variables such that

x
does not occur bound in
\varphi
and
y
does not occur in
\varphi
.

Then

(i)

\vdash\,\forall x\varphi\to\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
;

(ii)

\vdash\,\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\forall x\varphi
;

(iii)

\vdash\,\forall x\varphi\leftrightarrow\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
.

Proof.

The case
x=y
is obvious, as
Subf^{x}_{x}{\varphi}=\varphi
. Assume in the sequel that
x
,
y

are distinct.

(i)

As, by hypothesis,
y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we have, by Lemma
D.0.13
,
that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(
\forall
-Quantifier)

(2)

\vdash\forall y\forall x\varphi\to\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(
2.131
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall y\forall x\varphi

(
2.138
) as
y
does not occur in
\forall x\varphi
, hence

y\notin FV(\forall x\varphi)

and (
2.18
)

(4)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(syllogism)
: (3), (2)

(ii)

Let us denote
\psi:=Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
. By hypothesis,
y
does not occur in
\varphi
. In particular,
y
does not occur bound
in
\varphi
. We can apply Lemma
D.0.5
to get that
y
does not occur bound in
\psi
.

By hypothesis,
x
does not occur bound in
\varphi
. We can apply Lemma
D.0.4
to get that

x
does not occur in
\psi
.

Apply (
i
) with
x:=y
,
y:=x
,
\varphi:=\psi
to get that

\vdash\forall y\psi\to\forall xSubf^{y}_{x}{\psi},

that is

\vdash\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\forall xSubf^{y}_{x}{Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}}.

As, by hypothesis,
y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we can apply Lemma
D.0.7
to get

Subf^{y}_{x}{Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}}=\varphi
. Thus, (
ii
) follows.

(iii)

Apply (
i
), (
ii
), the definition of
\leftrightarrow
and (
2.18
).

∎

2.1.3
Application

Proposition 2.1.25
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\gamma

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\gamma},

(2.140)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\chi},

(2.141)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\gamma

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\varphi}\cdot{\gamma}.

(2.142)

Proof.

(
2.140
):

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

(Assumption)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

(
2.18
): (1) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(3)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}

(framing-left)
: (2)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\gamma

(Assumption)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

(
2.18
): (4) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(6)

\Gamma\vdash{\psi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}

(framing-right)
: (5)

(7)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}

(syllogism)
: (3), (6)

(8)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

(Assumption)

(9)

\Gamma\vdash\psi\to\varphi

(
2.18
): (9) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(10)

\Gamma\vdash{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\gamma}

(framing-left)
: (9)

(11)

\Gamma\vdash\gamma\to\chi

(
2.18
): (4) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(12)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\gamma}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}

(framing-right)
: (11)

(13)

\Gamma\vdash{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}

(syllogism)
: (10), (12)

(14)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}

(
2.18
): (7), (13) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(
2.141
) We can apply (
2.140
) with
\gamma:=\chi
, as

\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\chi
, by (
2.92
).

(
3.72
) Apply (
2.140
) with
\psi:=\varphi
, as

\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi
, by (
2.92
).

∎

2.1.4
A congruence

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Proposition 2.1.26
.

Define the binary relation
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
on the set of patterns as follows: for all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle{\varphi}\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}\,{\psi}\quad\text{iff}\quad\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi.

Then
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is a congruence that is also an equivalence relation.

Proof.

We have that:

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\neg
, by (
2.118
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\vee
, by (
2.121
) and (
2.122
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\wedge
, by (
2.123
) and (
2.124
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\to
, by (
2.119
) and (
2.120
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\forall
, by (
2.132
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\exists
, by (
2.134
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\cdot
, by (
2.141
) and
(
2.142
).

Thus,
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is a congruence.

Furthermore,

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is reflexive, by (
2.92
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is symmetric, by (
2.83
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is transitive, by (
2.84
).

Thus,
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is also an equivalence relation.
∎

2.1.5
Replacement theorems

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Theorem 2.1.27

(Replacement Theorem for contexts)
.

For any context
C_{\Box}
and any patterns
\varphi,\psi
,

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
implies
\Gamma\vdash C_{\Box}[\varphi]\leftrightarrow C_{\Box}[\psi]
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.1.26
and
Theorem
D.0.1
.
∎

Theorem 2.1.28

(Replacement Theorem)
.

Let
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta
be patterns such that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\chi
and
\theta
is obtained
from
\chi
by replacing one or more occurrences of
\varphi
with
\psi
. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
implies
\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\theta
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.1.26
and
Theorem
D.0.2
.
∎

2.1.6
Bounded substitution theorem

Theorem 2.1.29
.

For any pattern
\varphi
and any variables
x
,
y
such that
y
does not occur in
\varphi

\displaystyle\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.1.26
,
Lemma
2.1.24
.(
iii
)
and Theorem
D.0.3
.
∎

2.1.7
Application more

Lemma 2.1.30
.

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle{(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})

\displaystyle\quad\text{if~}x\notin FV(\psi),

(2.143)

\displaystyle{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\psi}\cdot{\varphi})

\displaystyle\quad\text{if~}x\notin FV(\psi).

(2.144)

Proof.

Assume that
x\notin FV(\psi)
and let
y
be a new variable, distinct from
x

and not occuring in
\psi
. Let us denote
\delta:=Subb^{x}_{y}{\psi}
.
By Lemma
D.0.9
, we have that

x
does not occur in
\delta
.

(
2.143
):

(1)

\vdash{(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\delta}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\delta})

(Propagation
∃
)

(2)

\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\delta

Theorem
2.1.29

(3)

\vdash\bigg({(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})\bigg)\leftrightarrow\bigg({(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\delta}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\delta})\bigg)

Theorem
2.1.28
: (2)

(4)

\vdash\bigg({(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\delta}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\delta})\bigg)\to\bigg({(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})\bigg)

(
2.81
): (3)

(5)

\vdash{(\exists x\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\exists x({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})

(modus ponens)
: (1), (4)

(
2.144
):

(1)

\vdash{\delta}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\delta}\cdot{\varphi})

(Propagation
∃
)

(2)

\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\delta

Theorem
2.1.29

(3)

\vdash\bigg({\psi}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\psi}\cdot{\varphi})\bigg)\leftrightarrow\bigg({\delta}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\delta}\cdot{\varphi})\bigg)

Theorem
2.1.28
: (2)

(4)

\vdash\bigg({\delta}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\delta}\cdot{\varphi})\bigg)\to\bigg({\psi}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\psi}\cdot{\varphi})\bigg)

(
2.81
): (3)

(5)

\vdash{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x\varphi)}\to\exists x({\psi}\cdot{\varphi})

∎

2.2
Equivalence of the proof systems
\mathcal{G}^{c}
and
\mathcal{H}

In the sequel, we prove that the proof system
\mathcal{G}^{c}

is equivalent with the proof system
\mathcal{H}
, given in Appendix
B
.

2.2.1
Some theorems and derived rules in
\mathcal{H}

In this section we write simply
\vdash
instead of
\vdash_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Propositional connectives

In the following,
\Gamma
is an arbitrary set of patterns and
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\chi
are arbitrary patterns.

Proposition 2.2.1
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\psi\to\chi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\chi,

(2.145)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\vee\varphi\to\chi\vee\psi,

(2.146)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\chi,

(2.147)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\to(\varphi\to\chi),

(2.148)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\chi\to\varphi)\to(\chi\to\psi),

(2.149)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

imply

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\gamma).

(2.150)

):

(axiom-1)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\psi\to\chi

(Assumption)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)

(modus-ponens)
: (3), (2)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash(\varphi\to\psi)\to(\varphi\to\chi)

(modus-ponens)
: (4), (1)

(6)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

(Assumption)

(7)

(modus-ponens)
: (6), (5)

(

):

(1)

(3)

(4)

(axiom-2)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash(\neg\chi\to\varphi)\to(\neg\chi\to\psi)

(6)

(axiom-or-1)

(7)

): (6), (5)

(axiom-or-2)

(9)

(
2.145
): (7), (8)

):

(1)

\Gamma\vdash(\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi))\to((\varphi\to\psi)\to(\varphi\to\chi))

(axiom-2)

(2)

(Assumption)

(modus-ponens)
: (2), (1)

(4)

(Assumption)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\chi

(

):

(1)

(axiom-2)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)

(Assumption)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash(\varphi\to\psi)\to(\varphi\to\chi)

(modus-ponens)
: (2), (1)

(4)

(axiom-2)

\to((\psi\to(\varphi\to\psi))\to(\psi\to(\varphi\to\chi)))

\vdash(\neg\neg\psi\to\neg(\neg\varphi\vee\neg\psi))\to(\psi\to\neg(\neg\varphi\vee\neg\psi))

(
2.154
): (8)

First-order connectives

Proposition 2.2.13
.

For every pattern
\varphi
and element variables
x,y
such that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
,

\displaystyle\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

(2.175)

Proof.

(1)

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{\neg\varphi}\to\exists x\neg\varphi

(
\exists
-Quantifier)
, as x is free for y in
\neg\varphi

(2)

\vdash\neg\exists x\neg\varphi\to\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\neg\varphi}

(
2.156
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\neg\exists x\neg\varphi

(axiom-forall-1)

(4)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\neg\varphi}

(
2.145
): (3), (2)

(5)

\vdash\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\neg\varphi}\to\neg\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

by the
definition by recursion of
Subf^{x}_{y}

(6)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\neg\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(
2.145
): (4), (5)

(7)

\vdash\neg\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(axiom-3)

(8)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(
2.145
): (6), (7)

∎

.

∎

Chapter 3
Applicative matching logic with application and definedness
appML_{def}

The logic
appML_{def}
is defined as follows:

(i)

\mathcal{L}_{appML_{def}}
:

DEF\in\Sigma
(
DEF
is called the
definedness symbol
)

\mathcal{P}_{C}=\{\bot\}
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\{\neg\}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\{\to,\wedge,\vee,\cdot\}
,

\mathcal{Q}=\{\forall,\exists\}
,
Equal={\overline{\mathcal{Q}}}=SVar=\emptyset
.

(ii)

Its proof system,
\mathcal{T}_{def}
, is given in Appendix
C
.

We write simply
\vdash
instead of
\vdash_{\mathcal{T}_{def}}
. As in Chapter
2
, we use the infix notation for patterns: we write
\varphi\to\psi
,
\varphi\vee\psi
,
\varphi\wedge\psi
,

{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}
.

We introduce the derived connective
\leftrightarrow
,
\left\lceil\cdot\right\rceil
,
\left\lfloor\cdot\right\rfloor
,
\mathbf{=}
by the following abbreviations:

\displaystyle\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

\displaystyle:=(\varphi\to\psi)\wedge(\psi\to\varphi),

\displaystyle\left\lceil{\varphi}\right\rceil

\displaystyle:={DEF}\cdot{\varphi},

\displaystyle\left\lfloor{\varphi}\right\rfloor

\displaystyle:=\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil,

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi

\displaystyle:=\left\lfloor\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\right\rfloor.

For every pattern
\varphi
,
\left\lceil{\varphi}\right\rceil
is called the
definedness of
\varphi
and

\left\lfloor{\varphi}\right\rfloor
is called the
totality of
\varphi
.

We use the same conditions on the precedence of connectives/quantifiers as in Chapter
2
. We assume, moreover, that

\mathbf{=}
has higher precedence than
\rightarrow
,
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\leftrightarrow
.

3.1
Theorems and derived rules in
\mathcal{T}_{def}
- I

In the following,
\Gamma
is an arbitrary set of patterns and
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\chi
are arbitrary patterns.

Lemma 3.1.7

(6)

(

) :

(1)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\leftrightarrow\neg\exists x\neg\varphi

(

Lemma 3.1.8
.

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi)\to\forall x\varphi\wedge\forall x\psi,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\forall x\varphi\wedge\forall x\psi\to\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\forall x\varphi\wedge\forall x\psi\leftrightarrow\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi).

(3.23)

Proof.

(
3.21
):

(1)

\vdash\varphi\wedge\psi\to\varphi

(weakening)

(2)

\vdash\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi)\to\forall x\varphi

(
3.10
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\varphi\wedge\psi\to\psi

(
2.2
)

(4)

\vdash\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi)\to\forall x\psi

(
3.10
): (3)

(5)

\vdash\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi)\to\forall x\psi\wedge\forall x\varphi

(
2.22
): (3), (4)

(
3.22
):

(1)

\vdash\varphi\to(\psi\to\varphi\wedge\psi)

(
2.5
)

(2)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall x(\psi\to\varphi\wedge\psi)

(
3.10
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\forall x(\psi\to\varphi\wedge\psi)\to(\forall x\psi\to\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi))

(fol-axiom-1)

(4)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to(\forall x\psi\to\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi))

(syllogism)
: (2), (3)

(5)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\wedge\forall x\psi\to\forall x(\varphi\wedge\psi)

(importation)
: (5)

(
3.23
) follows from (
3.21
),
(
3.22
),
the definition of
\leftrightarrow
and (
2.18
).

3.15
)

\vdash\exists x\varphi\to\neg\neg\varphi

(syllogism)

(5)

\vdash\neg\neg\varphi\to\varphi

(
2.82
) and (
2.95
)

(6)

\vdash\exists x\varphi\to\varphi

: (4), (5)

Lemma 3.1.10
.

Let
z
be a variable such that
z
does not occur in
\chi
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi),

(3.25)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\leftrightarrow\forall z\psi),

(3.26)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\exists z\varphi\to\exists z\psi),

(3.27)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\exists z\varphi\leftrightarrow\exists z\psi).

(3.28)

Proof.

):

(Assumption)

(2)

): (1)

(3)

(4)

(syllogism)
: (2), (3)

(5)

(fol-axiom-2)
, as
z
does not occur in
\chi

(6)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi)

(syllogism)
: (5), (4)

(

):

(Assumption)

(2)

(weakening)
and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi)

(
3.25
): (3)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\psi\to\varphi)

(
2.2
) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(6)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\psi\to\varphi)

(syllogism)
: (4), (5)

(7)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\psi\to\forall z\varphi)

(
3.25
): (6)

(8)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\leftrightarrow\forall z\psi)

(
2.22
): (4), (7)

(
3.27
):

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

(Assumption)

(2)

)

(3)

(

): (3)

(

)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\neg\forall z\neg\varphi\to\neg\forall z\neg\psi)

(

): (7), (8)

Lemma 3.1.11
.

Let
z
be a variable such that
z
does not occur free in
\chi
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi),

(3.29)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\leftrightarrow\forall z\psi),

(3.30)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\exists z\varphi\to\exists z\psi),

(3.31)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\exists z\varphi\leftrightarrow\exists z\psi).

(3.32)

Proof.

Assume that
z\notin FV(\chi)
and let
y
be a new variable, distinct from
z

and not occuring in
\chi
. Let us denote
\delta:=Subb^{z}_{y}{\chi}
.
By Lemma
D.0.9
, we have that

z
does not occur in
\delta
.

(
3.29
):

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi)

(Assumption)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\delta

Theorem
3.2.6

(3)

\Gamma\vdash(\chi\to(\varphi\to\psi))\leftrightarrow(\delta\to(\varphi\to\psi))

Theorem
3.1.32
: (2)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\delta\to(\varphi\to\psi)

(
2.81
): (1), (3)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash\delta\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi)

(
3.25
): (4)

(6)

\Gamma\vdash(\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi))\leftrightarrow(\delta\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi))

Theorem
3.1.32
: (2)

(7)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to(\forall z\varphi\to\forall z\psi)

(
2.81
): (5), (6)

(
3.30
) - (
3.32
) are proved similary, using
(
3.26
) - (
3.28
).
∎

(5)

Lemma 3.1.14
.

\displaystyle\vdash\,\neg\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\leftrightarrow\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\vdash\,\neg\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\vdash\,\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\neg\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor.

(3.40)

Proof.

By definition,
\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor=\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil
, hence
\neg\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor=\neg\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil
.
Apply (
2.95
) to get (
3.38
).

As, by definition,
\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor=\neg\left\lceil\neg\neg\varphi\right\rceil
, we get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\neg\neg\varphi

(
2.95
)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\left\lceil\neg\neg\varphi\right\rceil

(
3.35
): (1)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\neg\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor

(
2.67
): (2)

Hence, (
3.39
) holds. Furthermore,

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\neg\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor

(
3.39
)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\neg\neg\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\neg\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor

(
2.67
): (1)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\neg\neg\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(
2.95
)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\neg\left\lfloor\neg\varphi\right\rfloor

(
2.84
): (3), (2)

Hence, (
3.40
) also holds.
∎

Lemma 3.1.17
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n},\psi
be patterns. The following are equivalent:

(i)

(
\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{1}
,
\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{2}
, …,
\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{n}
)
imply
\Gamma\vdash\psi
;

(ii)

(
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi_{1}\right\rfloor
,
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi_{2}\right\rfloor
, …,
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi_{n}\right\rfloor
)
imply
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\psi\right\rfloor
.

Proof.

By (
3.42
), we have that (for all
i=1,\ldots,n
,
\Gamma\vdash\varphi_{i}
iff

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi_{i}\right\rfloor
) and (
\Gamma\vdash\psi
iff
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\psi\right\rfloor
).
∎

Lemma 3.1.18

)

(5)

\vdash\left\lceil\neg(\varphi\wedge\psi)\right\rceil\to\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil\vee\left\lceil\neg\psi\right\rceil

(syllogism)
: (3), (4)

(6)

\vdash\neg(\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil\vee\left\lceil\neg\psi\right\rceil)\to\neg\left\lceil\neg(\varphi\wedge\psi)\right\rceil

(
2.67
): (5)

(7)

\vdash\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil\wedge\neg\left\lceil\neg\psi\right\rceil\to\neg\left\lceil\neg(\varphi\wedge\psi)\right\rceil

(syllogism)
: (1), (6)

(8)

\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\wedge\left\lfloor\psi\right\rfloor\to\left\lfloor\varphi\wedge\psi\right\rfloor

definition of
\left\lfloor\cdot\right\rfloor

(
3.48
): Apply (
3.46
), (
3.47
),
(
2.18
) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow
.
∎

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\left\lceil x\vee\varphi\right\rceil.

(2)

(3)

(4)

)

Lemma 3.1.25
.

Let
x
,

, be variables.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\to\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to({\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}),

)-(

.

3.1.4
Application

Proposition 3.1.26
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\text{and}\,\,\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\gamma

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\gamma},

(3.70)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\chi},

(3.71)

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\varphi}\cdot{\gamma}.

\leftrightarrow

(3)

(framing-left)
: (2)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\gamma

(Assumption)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash(\chi\leftrightarrow\gamma)\to(\chi\to\gamma)

(weakening)
and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(6)

\Gamma\vdash\chi\to\gamma

(modus-ponens)

(7)

\Gamma\vdash{\psi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}

(framing-right)
: (6)

(8)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}

(syllogism)
: (3), (7)

(9)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

(10)

): (9) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(11)

(12)

(Assumption)

(13)

(weakening)
and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(14)

\Gamma\vdash\gamma\to\chi

(modus-ponens)
: (12), (13)

(15)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\gamma}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}

(framing-right)
: (14)

(16)

\Gamma\vdash{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}

(syllogism)
: (11), (15)

(17)

\Gamma\vdash{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\gamma}

(
2.18
): (8), (16) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(

).

(

).
∎

3.1.5
Two congruences

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Proposition 3.1.29
.

Define the binary relation
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
on the set of patterns as follows: for all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle{\varphi}\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}\,{\psi}\quad\text{iff}\quad\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi.

Then
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is a congruence that is also an equivalence relation.

Proof.

We have that:

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\neg
, by (
2.118
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\vee
, by (
2.121
) and (
2.122
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\wedge
, by (
2.123
) and (
2.124
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\to
, by (
2.119
) and (
2.120
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\forall
, by (
3.11
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\exists
, by (
3.14
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\cdot
, by (
3.71
) and (
3.72
).

Thus,
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is a congruence.

Furthermore,

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is reflexive, by (
2.92
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is symmetric, by (
2.83
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is transitive, by (
2.84
).

Thus,
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is also an equivalence relation.
∎

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Proposition 3.1.30
.

Define the binary relation
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
on the set of patterns as follows: for all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle{\varphi}\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}{\psi}\quad\text{iff}\quad\Gamma\vdash\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi.

Then
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is a congruence that is also an equivalence relation.

Proof.

We have that:

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\neg
, by (
3.55
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\vee
, by (
3.56
) and (
3.57
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\wedge
, by (
3.58
) and (
3.59
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\to
, by (
3.60
) and (
3.61
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\forall
, by (
3.62
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\exists
, by (
3.63
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is compatible with
\cdot
, by (
3.64
) and (
3.65
).

Thus,
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is a congruence.

Furthermore,

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is reflexive, by (
3.52
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is symmetric, by (
3.53
).

\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is transitive, by (
3.54
).

Thus,
\textbf{Cong}_{\Gamma}
is also an equivalence relation.
∎

3.1.6
Replacement theorems

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Theorem 3.1.31

(Replacement Theorem for contexts for
\leftrightarrow
)
.

For any context
C_{\Box}
and any patterns
\varphi,\psi
,

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
implies
\Gamma\vdash C_{\Box}[\varphi]\leftrightarrow C_{\Box}[\psi]
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.1.29
and Theorem
D.0.1
.
∎

Theorem 3.1.32

(Replacement Theorem for
\leftrightarrow
)
.

Let
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta
be patterns such that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\chi
and
\theta
is obtained
from
\chi
by replacing one or more occurrences of
\varphi
with
\psi
. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
implies
\Gamma\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow\theta
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.1.29
and Theorem
D.0.2
.
∎

Theorem 3.1.33

(Replacement Theorem for contexts for
\mathbf{=}
)
.

For any context
C_{\Box}
and any patterns
\varphi,\psi
,

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi
implies
\Gamma\vdash C_{\Box}[\varphi]\mathbf{=}C_{\Box}[\psi]
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.1.30
and Theorem
D.0.1
.
∎

Theorem 3.1.34

(Replacement Theorem for
\mathbf{=}
)
.

Let
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta
be patterns such that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\chi
and
\theta
is obtained
from
\chi
by replacing one or more occurrences of
\varphi
with
\psi
. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi
implies
\Gamma\vdash\chi\mathbf{=}\theta
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.1.30
and Theorem
D.0.2
.
∎

3.2
Theorems and derived rules in
\mathcal{T}_{def}
- II

3.2.1
Free substitution

Proposition 3.2.1
.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns and
x
,
y
be element variables such that

(i)

x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
;

(ii)

\psi
is obtained from
\varphi
by replacing a free occurence of
x
in
\varphi
with
y
.

Then

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi).

Proof.

Assume first that
x=y
. Then
x
is free for
x
in
\varphi
(by Lemma
D.0.12
)
and
\psi=\varphi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi

(
2.92
)

(2)

\vdash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi)\to(x\mathbf{=}x\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi))

(
2.3
)

(3)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}x\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi)

(modus ponens)
: (1), (2)

Assume in the sequel that
x
,
y
are distinct.

If
x
does not occur free in
\varphi
, then
\psi=\varphi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi

(
2.92
)

(2)

\vdash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi)\to(x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi))

(
2.3
)

(3)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi)

(modus ponens)
: (1), (2)

Assume in the following that
x
occurs free in
\varphi
. The proof is by induction on
\varphi
.

(i)

\varphi
is an atomic pattern. As
x
occurs free in
\varphi
, we must have
\varphi=x
. Obviously,

x
is free for
y
in
x
. Thus,

\psi=y
.
Then, by (
3.50
),
\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(x\leftrightarrow y)
, that is

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi).

(ii)

\varphi=\neg\chi
. As
x
occurs free in
\varphi
, we have that
x
occurs free in
\chi
.
As
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, we have that
x
is free for
y
in
\chi
. Furthermore,

\psi=\neg\delta
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\chi
by replacing a free occurence of

x
in
\chi
with
y
. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\chi

(2)

\vdash(\chi\leftrightarrow\delta)\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
2.111
)

(3)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(iii)

\varphi=\chi\circ\sigma
, where
\circ\in\{\to,\wedge,\vee\}
.
As
x
occurs free in
\varphi
, we have that
x
occurs free in
\chi
or
x
occurs free in
\sigma
.
As
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, we have that
x
is free for
y
in
\chi
and
x
is free for
y
in
\sigma
.
We have two cases:

(a)

\psi
is obtained from
\varphi
by replacing a free occurence of
x
in
\chi
with
y
. Then

\psi=\delta\circ\sigma
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\chi
by replacing a free occurence of

x
in
\chi
with
y
. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\chi

(2)

\vdash(\chi\leftrightarrow\delta)\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
2.112
) if
\circ=\to
;
(
2.114
) if
\circ=\vee
; (
2.116
) if
\circ=\wedge

(3)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(b)

\psi
is obtained from
\varphi
by replacing a free occurence of
x
in
\sigma
with
y
. Then

\psi=\chi\circ\delta
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma
by replacing a free occurence
of
x
in
\sigma
with
y
. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\sigma\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma

(2)

\vdash(\sigma\leftrightarrow\delta)\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
2.113
) if
\circ=\to
;
(
2.115
) if
\circ=\vee
; (
2.117
) if
\circ=\wedge

(3)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(iv)

\varphi={\chi}\cdot{\sigma}
. As
x
occurs free in
\varphi
, we have that
x
occurs free in

\chi
or
x
occurs free in
\sigma
. As
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, we have that
x
is
free for
y
in
\chi
and
x
is free for
y
in
\sigma
.
We have two cases:

(a)

\psi
is obtained from
\varphi
by replacing a free occurence of
x
in
\chi
with
y
. Then

\psi={\delta}\cdot{\sigma}
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\chi
by replacing a free occurence
of
x
in
\chi
with
y
. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\chi

(2)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
3.67
): (1)

(b)

\psi
is obtained from
\varphi
by replacing a free occurence of
x
in
\sigma
with
y
. Then

\psi={\chi}\cdot{\delta}
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma
by replacing a free occurence
of
x
in
\sigma
with
y
. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\sigma\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma

(2)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
3.69
): (1)

(v)

\varphi=Qz\chi
, where
Q\in\{\forall,\exists\}
. As

,
we have that
x\neq z
and
x
occurs free in
\chi
. As

is free for

y
in
\varphi
, we must have that

, hence
x
is free for

in
\chi
.
Then
\psi=Qz\delta
, where
\delta

from

by replacing a free occurence of

in

with

. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\chi

(2)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
3.26
) or
(
3.28
): (1) with
\chi:=x\mathbf{=}y
, as
z\neq x,y

∎

Proposition 3.2.2

(Substitution of equal variables)
.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns and
x
,
y
be element variables such that

(i)

x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
;

(ii)

\psi
is obtained from
\varphi
by replacing one or more free occurences of
x
in
\varphi
with
y
.

Then

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi).

Proof.

Let
n
be the number of free occurences of
x
in
\varphi
that are replaced with
y
. The proof is by induction on
n
.

n=1
: Apply Proposition
3.2.1
.

n\Rightarrow n+1
: Let
\psi^{\prime}
be the pattern obtained by replacing
n
occurences of
x
in
\varphi
by
y
. Thus, we can apply the induction hypothesis
to get that

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}).

(3.79)

Remark that
\psi
is obtained from
\psi^{\prime}
by replacing one occurence of
x
in
\psi^{\prime}
with
y
.
Furthermore, as
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, obviously,
x
is free for
y
in
\psi^{\prime}
. Thus, we can apply Proposition
3.2.1

to get that

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to(\psi^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\psi).

(3.80)

Appply now (
2.109
), (
3.79
), (
3.80
) to get the conclusion.
∎

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and

,
y
be element variables such that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
.

Then

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to\left(\varphi\leftrightarrow Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\right).

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.2.2
.
∎

Proposition 3.2.4
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be distinct element variables such that

(i)

x
does not occur bound in
\varphi
;

(ii)

x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
.

Then

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

Proof.

D.0.4
, we have
that
x
does not occur in
Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
. We get that

(1)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to\left(\varphi\leftrightarrow Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\right)

Proposition
3.2.3

(2)

\vdash x\mathbf{=}y\to\left(\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\right)

(
2.22
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\varphi\to\left(x\mathbf{=}y\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\right)

(
2.29
): (2)

(4)

\vdash(x\mathbf{=}y\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi})\to(\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\neg(x\mathbf{=}y))

(
2.66
)

(5)

\vdash\varphi\to(\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\neg(x\mathbf{=}y))

(syllogism)
: (3), (4)

(6)

(

(7)

\vdash(\forall x\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\forall x\neg(x\mathbf{=}y))\to

(

)

\to(\neg\forall x\neg(x\mathbf{=}y)\to\neg\forall x\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi})

(8)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to(\neg\forall x\neg(x\mathbf{=}y)\to\neg\forall x\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi})

(syllogism)
: (6), (7)

(9)

\vdash\neg\forall x\neg(x\mathbf{=}y)\to(\forall x\varphi\to\neg\forall x\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi})

(

): (8)

(axiom-exists-1)

(syllogism)

(fol-axiom-3)

,

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be element variables such that

x
does not occur bound in
\varphi
and
y
does not occur in
\varphi
.

Then

(i)

\vdash\,\forall x\varphi\to\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
;

(ii)

\vdash\,\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\forall x\varphi
;

(iii)

\vdash\,\forall x\varphi\leftrightarrow\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
.

Proof.

The case
x=y
is obvious, as
Subf^{x}_{x}{\varphi}=\varphi
. Assume in the sequel that
x
,
y

are distinct.

(i)

As, by hypothesis,
y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we have, by Lemma
D.0.13
,
that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

Proposition
3.2.4

(2)

\vdash\forall y\forall x\varphi\to\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(
3.10
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall y\forall x\varphi

(fol-axiom-2)
, as
y
does not occur in
\forall x\varphi

(4)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(syllogism)
: (3), (2)

(ii)

Let us denote
\psi:=Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
. By hypothesis,
y
does not occur in
\varphi
. In particular,
y
does not occur bound
in
\varphi
. We can apply Lemma
D.0.5
to get that
y
does not occur bound in
\psi
.

By hypothesis,
x
does not occur bound in
\varphi
. We can apply Lemma
D.0.4
to get that

x
does not occur in
\psi
.

Apply (
i
) with
x:=y
,
y:=x
,
\varphi:=\psi
to get that

\vdash\forall y\psi\to\forall xSubf^{y}_{x}{\psi},

that is

\vdash\forall ySubf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\forall xSubf^{y}_{x}{Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}}.

As, by hypothesis,
y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we can apply Lemma
D.0.7
to get

Subf^{y}_{x}{Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}}=\varphi
. Thus, (
ii
) follows.

(iii)

Apply (
i
), (
ii
),
the definition of
\leftrightarrow
and (
2.18
).

∎

3.2.2
Bounded substitution theorem

Theorem 3.2.6
.

For any pattern
\varphi
and any variables
x
,
y
such that
y
does not occur in
\varphi

\displaystyle\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.1.29
,
Lemma
3.2.5
.(
iii
)
and Theorem
D.0.3
.
∎

3.2.3
Universal and existential specification

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be distinct element variables such that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
.

Then

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

Proof.

Let
z
be a new variable, different from
x
,
y
and not occuring in
\varphi
. We get, by Theorem
3.2.6
, that

\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}.

(3.81)

Apply (
3.81
) and (
3.11
) to get that

\vdash\forall x\varphi\leftrightarrow\forall xSubb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}

(3.82)

Let us denote
\psi:=Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}
. As
x\neq z
, we can apply Lemma
D.0.8

to get that

(*)
x
does not occur bound in
\psi
.

Furthermore, as
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, we can apply
Lemma
D.0.14
to get that

(**)
x
is free for
y
in
\psi
.

Using (*), (**) and the fact that
x\neq y
, an application of Proposition
3.2.4
gives us

\vdash\forall x\psi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\psi},

that is

\vdash\forall xSubb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}\to Subf^{x}_{y}{Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}

(3.83)

Let us denote
\chi:=Subf^{x}_{y}{Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}
. As
x\neq y,z
, we have, by Lemma
D.0.10
, that

x
does not occur in
\chi
. Thus, we can apply Theorem
3.2.6
to get that

\vdash\chi\leftrightarrow Subb^{z}_{x}{\chi},

that is,

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}\leftrightarrow Subb^{z}_{x}{Subf^{x}_{y}{Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}}.

As
x
,
y
,
z
are distinct element variables and
z
does not occur in
\varphi
, we have, by Lemma
D.0.11
, that

Subb^{z}_{x}{Subf^{x}_{y}{Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}}=Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
. It follows that

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{Subb^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}\leftrightarrow Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

(3.84)

Apply (
3.82
), (
3.83
) and (
3.84
) to conclude that

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

∎

Corollary 3.2.8
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be distinct element variables such that
y
does not occur in
\varphi
.

Then

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

Proof.

As
y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we have, by Lemma
D.0.4
,
that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
. Apply Proposition
3.2.7
.
∎

Theorem 3.2.9

(Universal Specification)
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be element variables such that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
.

Then

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}.

Proof.

Let
z
be a new variable, distinct from
x
,
y
and not occuring in
\varphi
.
We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}

Corollary
3.2.8
,
as
z\neq x
and
z
does not occur in
\varphi

(2)

\vdash\forall z\forall x\varphi\to\forall zSubf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}

(
3.10
): (1)

(3)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall z\forall x\varphi

(fol-axiom-2)
, as
z
does not
occur in
\forall x\varphi

(4)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\forall zSubf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}

(syllogism)
: (3), (2)

Let
\psi:=Subf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}
. As
z
does not occur in
\varphi
and, by hypothesis,
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, we get from
Lemma
D.0.15
that

(*)
z
is free for
y
in
\psi
.

By (*) and the fact that
z\neq y
, we can apply Proposition
3.2.7
to get that

\vdash\forall z\psi\to Subf^{z}_{y}{\psi}.

It follows that

(5)

\vdash\forall zSubf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}\to Subf^{z}_{y}{Subf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}}

by the definition of
\psi

(6)

\vdash\forall zSubf^{x}_{z}{\varphi}\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

by
Lemma
D.0.6
, as
z
does not occur in
\varphi

(7)

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(syllogism)
: (4), (6).

∎

Theorem 3.2.10

(Existential Specification)
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be element variables such that
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
.

Then

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\exists x\varphi.

Proof.

We have that

(1)

\vdash\forall x\neg\varphi\to Subf^{x}_{y}{\neg\varphi}

Theorem
3.2.9
, as
x
is free for
y
in
\neg\varphi

(2)

\vdash\forall x\neg\varphi\to\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

as
Subf^{x}_{y}{\neg\varphi}=\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(3)

\vdash\neg\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\neg\forall x\neg\varphi

(
2.67
): (2)

(4)

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\neg\neg Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

(
2.82
) and (
2.95
)

(5)

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\neg\forall x\neg\varphi

(syllogism)
: (4), (3)

(6)

\vdash\neg\forall x\neg\varphi\to\exists x\varphi

(axiom-exists-2)

(7)

\vdash Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\exists x\varphi

(syllogism)
: (5), (6)

∎

Lemma 3.2.12
.

\displaystyle\vdash

.
We get that

\vdash\forall x\varphi\to\varphi

)

(2)

\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

Theorem
3.2.6

(3)

\vdash Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\varphi

(
2.18
): (1) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow

(4)

\vdash\forall xSubb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\forall x\varphi

(

): (1) and the

\leftrightarrow

(syllogism)
: (7), (6)

(

)

): (2)

(4)

\vdash\forall x(\varphi\to\psi)\to(\varphi\to\forall x\psi)

(syllogism)
: (1), (3)

(gen)
: (1)

3.90
)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\forall x\psi

(modus ponens)
: (3), (2)

Lemma 3.2.18

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

)

: (2), (1)

3.2.5

Lemma 3.2.19
.

be a variable. Then

(3.100)

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,\forall x\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\leftrightarrow\left\lfloor\forall x\varphi\right\rfloor.

(3.101)

Proof.

(
3.98
):

(1)

\varphi\to\exists x\varphi

(
3.86
)

(2)

\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\to\left\lceil\exists x\varphi\right\rceil

(
3.34
): (1)

(3)

\exists x\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\to\left\lceil\exists x\varphi\right\rceil

(

x\notin FV(\left\lceil\exists x\varphi\right\rceil)

(
3.99
): Remark that, by definition,

\left\lceil\exists x\varphi\right\rceil={DEF}\cdot{\exists x\varphi}
and
\exists x\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil=\exists x({DEF}\cdot{\varphi})
.

As

does not occur in

, we can apply
(propagation
∃
)
with
\psi:=DEF
to get
(
3.99
).

(

) Apply (
3.98
),
(
3.99
) and the definition of
\leftrightarrow
.

Remark that, by definition,

\left\lfloor\forall x\varphi\right\rfloor=\neg\left\lceil\neg\forall x\varphi\right\rceil
and
\forall x\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor=\forall x\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil
.

(

):

3.100
)

(3)

(
2.118
): (2)

(4)

\vdash\neg\left\lceil\exists x\neg\varphi\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\forall x\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor

(syllogism)
: (3), (1)

(5)

\vdash\neg\forall x\varphi\leftrightarrow\exists x\neg\varphi

(
3.20
)

(6)

Lemma 3.2.22
.

\varphi
,
\psi
,

3.2.7
Substitutivity of equal patterns

Theorem 3.2.23
.

Let
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta
be patterns such that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\chi
and
\theta
is obtained
from
\chi
by replacing one occurrence of
\varphi
with
\psi
.
Let
x_{1},\ldots x_{n}
be variables with the following property:

(*)\quad(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\chi)\subseteq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}.

Then

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)
.

Proof.

If
\varphi=\chi
, then
\theta=\psi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi

(
3.97
)

(2)

\vdash\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(
3.50
)

(3)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

Assume in the sequel that
\varphi\neq\chi
. The proof is by induction on
\chi
.

(i)

\chi
is an atomic pattern. Then one must have
\varphi=\chi
.

(ii)

\chi=\neg\sigma
. As
\varphi\neq\chi
, we must have that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\sigma
.
Thus,
\theta=\neg\delta
, where

\delta
is obtained from
\sigma
by replacing an occurence of

\varphi
in
\sigma
with
\psi
. Furthermore,

(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\sigma)=(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\chi)\subseteq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}.

Hence, (*) holds for
\sigma
instead of
\chi
.
We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\sigma\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma

(2)

\vdash(\sigma\leftrightarrow\delta)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
2.111
)

(3)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(iii)

\chi=\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}
, where
\circ\in\{\to,\wedge,\vee\}
.
As
\varphi\neq\chi
, we must have that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\sigma_{1}
or that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\sigma_{2}
.
Furthermore, for all
i=1,2
,

(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\sigma_{i})\subseteq(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\chi)\subseteq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}.

Hence, (*) holds for
\sigma_{1}
or
\sigma_{2}
instead of
\chi
.

We have two cases:

(a)

\theta=\delta\circ\sigma_{2}
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma_{1}
by replacing
an occurence of
\varphi
in
\sigma_{1}
with
\psi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma_{1}

(2)

\vdash(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow\delta)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
2.112
) if
\circ=\to
;
(
2.114
) if
\circ=\vee
; (
2.116
) if
\circ=\wedge

(3)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(b)

\theta=\sigma_{1}\circ\delta
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma_{2}
by replacing
an occurence of
\varphi
in
\sigma_{2}
with
\psi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\sigma_{2}\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma_{2}

(2)

\vdash(\sigma_{2}\leftrightarrow\delta)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
2.113
) if
\circ=\to
;
(
2.115
) if
\circ=\vee
; (
2.117
) if
\circ=\wedge

(3)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(syllogism)
: (1), (2)

(iv)

\chi={\sigma_{1}}\cdot{\sigma_{2}}
.
As
\varphi\neq\chi
, we must have that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\sigma_{1}
or that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\sigma_{2}
.
Furthermore, for all
i=1,2
,

(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\sigma_{i})\subseteq(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\chi)\subseteq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}.

Hence, (*) holds for
\sigma_{1}
or
\sigma_{2}
instead of
\chi
.

We have two cases:

(a)

\theta={\delta}\cdot{\sigma_{2}}
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma_{1}
by replacing
an occurence of
\varphi
in
\sigma_{1}
with
\psi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi=\psi)\to(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma_{1}

(2)

\vdash\left\lfloor\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\right\rfloor\to(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow\delta)

(
2.89
): (
3.103
) and (1)

(3)

\vdash\left\lfloor\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\right\rfloor\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
3.76
): (2)

(4)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi=\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
2.89
): (
3.103
) and (3)

(b)

\theta={\sigma_{1}}\cdot{\delta}
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma_{2}
by replacing
an occurence of
\varphi
in
\sigma_{2}
with
\psi
. We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi=\psi)\to(\sigma_{2}\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma_{2}

(2)

\vdash\left\lfloor\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\right\rfloor\to(\sigma_{2}\leftrightarrow\delta)

(
2.89
): (
3.103
) and (1)

(3)

\vdash\left\lfloor\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\right\rfloor\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
3.78
): (2)

(4)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi=\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
2.89
): (
3.103
) and (3)

(v)

\chi=Qz\sigma
, where
Q\in\{\forall,\exists\}
. As
\varphi\neq\chi
, we must have that

\varphi
is a subpattern of
\sigma
.

Thus,
\theta=Qz\delta
, where
\delta
is obtained from
\sigma
by replacing an occurence of

\varphi
in
\sigma
with
\psi
.

Claim 1:
z\notin FV(\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi))
.

Proof of claim:
We have two cases:

(a)

z\notin FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi)
. Then the conclusion is obvious.

(b)

z\in FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi)
. As
z\in BV(\chi)
, we have that

z\in(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\chi)
.

Hence, by (*),
z\in\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. Thus,

z\notin FV(\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi))
.
\blacksquare

We get that

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\sigma\leftrightarrow\delta)

induction hypothesis on
\sigma
, as
BV(\sigma)\subseteq BV(\chi)
,

hence (*)
holds for
\sigma
instead of
\chi

(2)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(1) and
(
3.30
) if
Q=\forall
;
(
3.32
) if
Q=\exists

∎

Theorem 3.2.24

(Substitutivity of equal patterns)
.

Let
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta
be patterns such that
\varphi
is a subpattern of
\chi
and
\theta
is obtained
from
\chi
by replacing one or more occurrences of
\varphi
with
\psi
.
Let
x_{1},\ldots x_{n}
be variables with the following property:

(*)\quad(FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi))\cap BV(\chi)\subseteq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}.

Then

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the number
n
of occurrences of
\varphi
in
\chi
thar are replaced with
\psi
.

n=1
: Apply Theorem
3.2.23
.

n=n+1
: Let
\theta^{\prime}
be the pattern obtained by replacing
n
occurrences of
\varphi
in
\chi
with
\psi
. Then

\theta
is obtained from
\theta^{\prime}
by replacing one occurence of
\varphi
in
\theta^{\prime}
with
\psi
.
We get that:

(1)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta^{\prime})

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\theta^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\theta)

Theorem
3.2.23

(3)

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)

(
2.109
): (1), (2)

∎

The following is an immediate corollary of Theorem
3.2.24
.

Proposition 3.2.25
.

Let
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta
be patterns such that
\varphi
is a
subpattern of
\chi
and
\theta
is obtained from
\chi
by replacing one or more occurrences of
\varphi
with
\psi
.

(i)

If
\chi
is quantifier-free, then

\vdash\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)
.

(ii)

Let
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}=FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi)
.
Then

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)
.

(iii)

Let
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}=BV(\chi)
.
Then

\vdash\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall x_{n}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\chi\leftrightarrow\theta)
.

3.2.8
A deduction theorem

We prove a deduction theorem similar with
[
2
, Theorem 4.2]
, the deduction theorem
for matching
\mu
-logic.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
be a set of patterns. For every pattern
\psi
, let us denote

(*)

\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash^{*}\psi

iff

there exists a
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
-proof of
\psi

that contains no applications of

(gen)
involving a variable which occurs free in
\varphi
.

Theorem 3.2.26

(Deduction Theorem)
.

Assume that
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash^{*}\psi
. Then
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}=\psi
be a
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
-proof
of
\psi
as in (*). We prove by induction on
i
that for all
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{i}.

As a consequence,
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{n}=\psi
.

i=1
: We have the following cases:

(i)

\theta_{1}
is an axiom or
\theta_{1}\in\Gamma
. We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\theta_{1}

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\theta_{1}\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\theta_{1})

(
2.3
)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\rightarrow\theta_{1}

(modus ponens)
: (1), (2)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\varphi

(
3.41
)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{1}

(syllogism)
: (4), (5)

(ii)

\theta_{1}=\varphi
. Apply (
3.41
).

Assume that the induction hypothesis is true for all
j=1,\ldots,i
. We have the following cases for
\theta_{i+1}
:

(i)

\theta_{i+1}
is an axiom or
\theta_{i+1}\in\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
. Then, as in the case
i=1
,

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\varphi_{i+1}
.

(ii)

\theta_{i+1}
is obtained by applying a propositional deduction rule.

(a)

(modus ponens)
: There exist
j,k<i+1
such that
\theta_{k}=\theta_{j}\to\theta_{i+1}
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{k}

(Induction hypothesis)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{i+1}

(
2.34
): (1), (2)

(b)

(syllogism)
: There exist
j,k<i+1
and patterns
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\chi
such that

\theta_{j}=\alpha\to\beta
,
\theta_{k}=\beta\to\chi
and
\theta_{i+1}=\alpha\to\chi
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{k}

(Induction hypothesis)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{i+1}

(
2.53
): (1), (2)

(c)

(exportation)
: There exists
j<i+1
and patterns
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\chi
such that

\theta_{j}=\alpha\wedge\beta\to\chi
and
\theta_{i+1}=\alpha\to(\beta\to\chi)
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{i+1}

(
2.56
): (1)

(d)

(importation)
: There exists
j<i+1
and patterns
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\chi
such that

\theta_{j}=\alpha\to(\beta\to\chi)
and
\theta_{i+1}=\alpha\wedge\beta\to\chi
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{i+1}

(
2.56
): (1)

(e)

(expansion)
: There exists
j<i+1
and patterns
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\chi
such that

\theta_{j}=\alpha\to\beta
and
\theta_{i+1}=\chi\vee\alpha\to\chi\vee\beta
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{i+1}

(
2.57
): (1)

(iii)

\theta_{i+1}
is obtained by applying
(gen)
that does not involve a variable which occurs free in
\varphi
.
Thus, there exists
j<i+1
such that
\theta_{i+1}=\forall x\theta_{j}
and
x\notin FV(\varphi)
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\forall x(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j})

(gen)
: (1)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\forall x(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j})\to(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\to\theta_{i+1})

(
3.90
), as
FV(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor)=FV(\varphi)
, so
x\notin FV(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\to\theta_{i+1}

(modus ponens)
: (2), (3)

(iv)

\theta_{i+1}
is obtained by applying one of the application deduction rules:

(a)

(framing-left)
: There exists
j<i+1
and patterns
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\chi
such that

\theta_{j}=\alpha\to\beta
and

\theta_{i+1}={\alpha}\cdot{\chi}\to{\beta}\cdot{\chi}
for some pattern
\chi
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\to\theta_{i+1}

(
3.75
): (1)

(b)

(framing-right)
: There exists
j<i+1
and patterns
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\chi
such that

\theta_{j}=\alpha\to\beta
and

\theta_{i+1}={\chi}\cdot{\alpha}\to{\chi}\cdot{\beta}
for some pattern
\chi
.

We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\theta_{j}

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\to\theta_{i+1}

(
3.76
): (1)

∎

Proposition 3.2.27
.

Assume that
\varphi
is a sentence. Then

\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash\psi
iff
\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) As
\varphi
is a sentence, we have that
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash\psi
iff
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash^{*}\psi
. Apply now
Theorem
3.2.26
.

(
\Leftarrow
) We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\rightarrow\psi

(Assumption)

(2)

\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi

(3)

\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash\varphi

(4)

\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\vdash\psi

(modus ponens)
: (2), (3)

∎

3.2.9
Application contexts

We refer to
[
11
, Section 2.9]
for the definition and basic properties of contexts that shall be studied
in this section, referred to as
application contexts
.
We prove for
appML_{def}
properties of application contexts that were obtained in

[
2
, pp. 46-48, 53]
for matching
\mu
-logic. We remark the fact that some of these
properties (see
[
2
, Proposition 3.3, Lemma 3.14]
) are proved in
[
2
]
by using
the so-called
(singleton variable)
, an axiom in the proof system for matching
\mu
-logic
from
[
2
]
. Our proofs are simpler, as they do not need
(singleton variable)
.

In the following,
C
is an application context.

Lemma 3.2.28
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi\text{ implies }

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash C[\varphi]\to C[\psi],

(3.106)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\text{ implies }

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash C[\varphi]\leftrightarrow C[\psi].

(3.107)

Proof.

(
3.106
):
The proof is by induction on the application context
C
.

(i)

C=\Box
. The conclusions are obvious, as
\Box[\delta]=\delta
for every pattern
\delta
.

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\chi
where
C_{1}
is an application context. Then
C[\delta]={C_{1}[\delta]}\cdot{\chi}

for every pattern
\delta
. We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

(Assumption)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash C_{1}[\varphi]\to C_{1}[\psi]

(Induction hypothesis): (1)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash C[\varphi]\to C[\psi]

(framing-left)
: (2)

(iii)

If C =
Appl_{\Box}\chi C_{1}
where
C_{1}
is an application context. Then
C[\delta]={\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\delta]}

for every pattern
\delta
. We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

(Assumption)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash C_{1}[\varphi]\to C_{1}[\psi]

(Induction hypothesis): (1)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash C[\varphi]\to C[\psi]

(framing-right)
: (2)

(
3.107
): Apply (
3.106
) and (
2.82
).
∎

Lemma 3.2.29
.

\displaystyle\vdash\,C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil.

(3.108)

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the application context
C
.

(i)

C=\Box
. Obviously, as
\Box[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]=\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil
.

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi
where
C_{1}
is an application context.
Then
C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]={C_{1}[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]}\cdot{\psi}
. We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash C_{1}[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}\cdot{\psi}

(framing-left)
: (2)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}\cdot{\psi}\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(Propagation
⌈⋅⌉
)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(syllogism)
: (2), (3)

(iii)

If C =
Appl_{\Box}\chi C_{1}
where
C_{1}
is an application context.
Then
C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]={\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]}
. We get that

(1)

\Gamma\vdash C_{1}[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(Induction hypothesis)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to{\psi}\cdot{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}

(framing-right)
: (2)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash{\psi}\cdot{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(Propagation
⌈⋅⌉
)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash C[\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(syllogism)
: (2), (3)

∎

Lemma 3.2.30
.

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,C[\varphi]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil.

(3.109)

Proof.

(1)

\vdash\varphi\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(def-pattern)

(2)

(
3.106
): (1)

(3)

(
3.108
)

(4)

\vdash C[\varphi]\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

(syllogism)
: (2), (3)

Lemma 3.2.33
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi

implies

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\neg C[\neg\varphi].

(3.115)

Proof.

(1)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi

(Assumption)

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\neg\neg\varphi

(
2.82
) and (
2.95
)

(3)

\Gamma\vdash\neg\neg\varphi

(modus-ponens)
: (1), (2)

(4)

\Gamma\vdash\neg\neg\varphi\to(\neg\varphi\to\bot)

(axiom-not-1)

(5)

(modus-ponens)
: (3), (4)

3.106
): (5)

(
3.110
)

(8)

\Gamma\vdash C[\bot]\to\bot

2.82
): (6)

(9)

: (6), (8)

(10)

\Gamma\vdash(C[\neg\varphi]\to\bot)\to\neg C[\neg\varphi]

(axiom-not-2)

(11)

\Gamma\vdash\neg C[\neg\varphi]

(modus-ponens)
: (9), (10)

∎

3.2.10
Membership

The derived connective
\in
(called
membership
) is defined as follows: for every
variable
x
and pattern
\varphi
,

\displaystyle x\in\varphi

\displaystyle:=\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil.

We assume that
\in
has higher precedence than
\rightarrow
,
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\leftrightarrow
.

We give in the sequel proofs of theorems and derived rules for membership, by following the proofs from

[
2
, pp. 51-53]
for matching
\mu
-logic. Most of the proofs from
[
2
]
make use of the

(Singleton Variable)
axiom. We show that we can replace it with a simpler axiom,
(singleton-simple)
.

Lemma 3.2.34
.

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,(x\in\varphi)\leftrightarrow\left\lceil\varphi\wedge x\right\rceil.

(3.116)

Proof.

(1)

\vdash x\wedge\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi\wedge x

(
2.93
)

(2)

\vdash(x\in\varphi)\leftrightarrow\left\lceil\varphi\wedge x\right\rceil

(
3.35
): (1) and the definition of
\in

∎

Lemma 3.2.35
.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi

(4)

(existence)

(5)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi

(modus-ponens)
: (4), (3)

∎

Lemma 3.2.45
.

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\,x\in(\varphi\wedge\psi)\leftrightarrow x\in\varphi\wedge x\in\psi,

(3.146)

(3.147)

Theorem 3.4.1
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
be a set of patterns. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
implies
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
.

Proof.

Let us denote
\Theta=\{\varphi\in Pattern\mid\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\}
. We prove that

Thm(\Gamma)\subseteq\Theta
by induction on
\Gamma
-theorems.

Axioms and Notations:

We prove that if
\varphi
is an axiom or a notation, then
\varphi
is valid. Hence, one
can apply
[
11
, Remark 3.32(ii)]
to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
, that is
\varphi\in\Theta
.

Propositional:

Assume that
\varphi
is a propositional axiom or notation. One can easily see that
\varphi
is a tautology,
By
[
11
, Proposition 3.66]
,
\varphi
is valid.

First-order:

(fol-axiom-1)
: Apply
[

, Proposition 3.85]
.

[

, Proposition 3.84]
and the fact that if

x
does not occur in
\varphi
, then
x\notin FV(\varphi)
.

(fol-axiom-3)

[

, Proposition 6.8]
.

(axiom-exists-1)
,
(axiom-exists-2)
: Obviously, by
[
11
, Section 3.1]
.

Application:

(Propagation
∨
)
: Apply
[
11
, Proposition 3.90]
.

(Propagation
∃
)
: Apply
[
11
, Proposition 3.90]
and the fact that if

x
does not occur in

, then
x\notin FV(\psi)
.

(Propagation

, Proposition 6.9]
.

D.0.2
Comparison of proof systems

Let
\mathcal{P}_{1}=(Axm_{1},DedRules_{1})
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}=(Axm_{2},DedRules_{2})
be two proof systems for
appML
.

Proposition D.0.16
.

Assume that

(i)

\vdash_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}\varphi
for every axiom
\varphi\in Axm_{1}
,

(ii)

For every set
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{L}
-patterns,
{\Gamma}\!-\!Thm_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
is closed to

DedRules_{1}
.

Then
\mathcal{P}_{1}\lesssim\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Proof.

See
[
10
, Proposition 13.12]
.
∎

Proposition D.0.17
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{P}_{1}\sim\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

(ii)

\mathcal{P}_{1}\lesssim\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}\lesssim\mathcal{P}_{1}
,

Proof.

See
[
10
, Proposition 13.14]
.∎
